3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.37003pt\raise-32.17834pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.37003pt\raise-32.17834pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

For a closed subgroup
H
of
G
we have equivalences of functors (Green’s imprimitivity theorem)

-\rtimes_{r}H\simeq{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(-)\rtimes_{r}G:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}\ ,\quad-\rtimes_{\max}H\simeq{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(-)\rtimes_{\max}G:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}

(2.12)

If
G
is discrete, then this equivalence is induced by the composition of
-\rtimes_{?}H
applied to the unit of the
({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H},\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H})
-adjunction and the transformation
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-)\rtimes_{?}H\to-\rtimes_{?}G
induced on the level of convolution algebras by the extension by zero
C_{c}(H,\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(-))\to C_{c}(G,-)
(it is here where we use discreteness). In the general case the unit is induced by a Morita bimodule and therefore starts to exist in
L_{K_{G}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
.

If
G
is compact, or more generally, amenable, then the canonical transformation

-\rtimes_{\max}G\to-\rtimes_{r}G

is an equivalence.

An
instance of Green’s imprimitivity theorem for
H=\{e\}
is the equivalence
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\rtimes_{r}G
,
where we use that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\simeq{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})
.
Here we consider
C_{0}(G)
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
using the
G
-action by left translations.
The object
C_{0}(G)
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
has an additional action of
G
induced by right translations.
We thus get
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
and therefore

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\rtimes_{r}G
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
.
Let
\mathrm{triv}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
be the functor which equips an object with the trivial
G
-action.

Lemma 2.3
.

The Green’s imprimitivity equivalence refines to an equivalence

\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\rtimes_{r}G

(2.13)

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
.

Proof.

We consider the Hilbert space
L^{2}(G)
. The algebra
C_{0}(G)
acts
by multiplication operators. The action is covariant for the action of
G
on
the Hilbert space and on the algebra by left translations. By the universal property of the reduced crossed product we therefore get a homomorphism

C_{0}(G)\rtimes_{r}G\to K(L^{2}(G))

(2.14)

which turns out to be an isomorphism.

We let
\rho
denote the action on
G
on
L^{2}(G)
by right-multiplication.
We interpret
\rho
as a continuous homomorphism
G\to UM(K(L^{2}(G)))
.
We then observe that under the isomorphism (
2.14
) the
G
-action on

C_{0}(G)\rtimes_{r}G
induced by right-translations on
C_{0}(G)
goes to the action on
K(L^{2}(G))
induced by conjugation with
\rho
.
We view
K(L^{2}(G))
with this conjugation action as an object of
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We then have

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(K(L^{2}(G)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{fvdsvervsdfvfdv}}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(K(L^{2}(G)))))\ .

By stability the right-hand side is equivalent to
\mathrm{Res}_{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})
.
This implies that the object
\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}\mathrm{kk}^{G}(K(L^{2}(G)))
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
is equivalent to the object obtained by equipping
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}
with the trivial
G
-action.
∎

For
B
in
\mathrm{KK}
we have an equivalence of functors (also natural in
B
)

B\otimes(-\rtimes_{r}G)\simeq(\mathrm{Res}_{G}(B)\otimes-)\rtimes_{r}G:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}

(2.15)

which comes from a similar natural isomorphism
on the level of
C^{*}
-algebras.
Here it is relevant that we work with the minimal tensor products.
There is an analogous result for the maximal crossed produce involving maximal tensor products.

If
G
is compact, then we have an adjunction (the Green-Julg theorem)

\mathrm{Res}_{G}:\mathrm{KK}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{KK}^{G}:-\rtimes_{r}G

(2.16)

whose unit
A\to\mathrm{Res}_{G}(A)\rtimes_{r}G
is already defined on the level of algebras and given by
a\mapsto{\mathtt{const}}_{a}
.

Finally, if
G
is discrete, then we have an adjunction

-\rtimes_{\max}G:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{KK}:\mathrm{Res}_{G}

(2.17)

whose unit
A\mapsto\mathrm{Res}_{G}(A\rtimes_{\max}G)
is given by a morphism which already exists in
L_{K_{G}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
. It is given by the cocycle morphism
A\ni a\mapsto a\delta_{e}\in C_{c}(G,A)
.

For both adjunctions the counits also exist in
L_{K_{G}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
, and the adjunctions
are descended from this intermediate localization, see (
2.4
)

Let
{\mathbf{1}}:=\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the tensor unit of
\mathrm{KK}
.
The commutative algebra
{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{KK}}({\mathbf{1}},{\mathbf{1}})
in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Sp})
is the complex
K
-theory spectrum from homotopy theory usually denoted by
KU
. As a consequence, the category

\mathrm{KK}
is a
{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
-module in the category
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
of stable presentable
\infty
-categories. In particular, its mapping spectra are
KU
-modules in a canonical way.

The symmetric monoidal restriction functor
\mathrm{Res}_{G}:\mathrm{KK}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
turns
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
into a
\mathrm{KK}
- and hence into a
{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
-module and we have

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}\simeq\mathrm{Res}_{G}({\mathbf{1}})
.

Definition 2.4
.

We define the representation ring

R(G):={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}},{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}})

of
G
in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Mod}}(KU))
.

The category
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is naturally a module over
{\mathbf{Mod}}(R(G))
in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
, and
its mapping spectra are naturally
R(G)
-modules.

Remark 2.5
.

If
G
is compact, then
\pi_{0}R(G)
can be identified with the classical representation ring of
G
. In particluar, for any finite-dimensional representation
V
of
G
we get an element
[V]
in
\pi_{0}R(G)
.

∎

Example 2.6
.

If
H
is open in
G
and
G/H
is finite, then the functor
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}
has a left and a right adjoint which are both given by the functor
{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
and the units described above.
The identity of
{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}
identifies the left and the right adjoint and refines

\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}
to an iso-normed functor
(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H},\mathrm{Nm}^{G}_{H})
in the sense of
[
CSY22
, Def. 2.1.3]
. Since
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}
is symmetric monoidal and we have the projection formula
(
2.11
) the normed functor is actually
\otimes
-normed in the sense of

[
CSY22
, Def. 2.3.1]
.

By
[
CSY22
, Def. 2.1.11]
, for every two objects
A,B
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
we have an integration

\int_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A),\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A,B)

which sends

f:\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A)\to\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B)
to the composition

A\to{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Nm}^{G,-1}_{H}}}{{\simeq}}{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A))\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H}(f)}{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B))\to B\ ,

(2.18)

where the first map is the unit of the
(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H},{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H})
-adjunction, and the last map
is the counit of the
({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H},\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H})
-adjunction.
We want to understand this map explicitly in the case where
f
comes from an algebra homomorphism.

So changeing notation let
A,B
be in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and let
f:\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(A)\to\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B)
be an
H
-equivariant homomorphism.
Then we want to calculate
\int_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}}\mathrm{kk}^{H}(f)
.
The first three maps in the composition (
2.18
) are defined already on the level
of algebras and send
f
to the function

g\mapsto f(g^{-1}a)
.
The counit of the
({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}_{H},\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H})
-adjunction
only exists in the localization
L_{K_{G}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
explained in (
2.4
).
It can be described as follows. A special case of the
projection formula (
2.11
) provides an isomorphism

{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B))\cong C(G/H)\otimes B

in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We furthermore embed
C(G/H)\otimes B
into
K(L^{2}(G/H))\otimes B
,
where the first factor acts as multiplication operators.
The counit is then represented by the sequence of homomorphisms of
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras

{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}(B))\cong C(G/H)\otimes B\to K(L^{2}(G/H))\otimes B\xleftarrow{\simeq}B\ ,

(2.19)

where the right map is a corner embedding sending
b
in
B
to the operator
p\otimes b
, where

p
in
K(L^{2}(G/H))
is the projection to the constant functions on
G/H
.
This map is a
K_{G}
-equivalence and therefore invertible in
L_{K_{G}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{h}
. The integral of
f
is then represented (up to the last equivalence in (
2.19
)) by the homomorphism of
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras

A\to K(L^{2}(G/H))\otimes B
given by

A\ni a\mapsto\sum_{gH\in G/H}p_{gH}\otimes gf(g^{-1}a)\in K(L^{2}(G/H))\otimes B\ ,

where
p_{gH}
in
K(L^{2}(G/H))
is the projection onto the one-dimensional subspace of functions supported on
gH
.

If
f
is
G
-invariant, then we read off that

\int_{\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}}\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}(f):A\xrightarrow{a\mapsto 1_{K(L^{2}(G/H))}\otimes f(a)}K(L^{2}(G/H))\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\leftarrow}}B\ .

The zig-zag of homomorphisms

{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{1\mapsto 1_{K(L^{2}(G/H))}}K(L^{2}(G/H))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\leftarrow}}{\mathbb{C}}

represents the class
\rho_{G/H}
in the representation ring
\pi_{0}R(G)
given by the representation of
G
on
L^{2}(G/H)
. So
\int_{\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}}\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}(f)\simeq\rho_{G/H}f
.
In particular, using the notation of
[
CSY22
, Def. 2..13]
, we have

\int_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}}{\mathtt{id}}_{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}}(A)\simeq|\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}|_{A}\simeq\rho_{G/H}\ .

∎

For certain applications the equivariant
KK
-theory functor (
2.1
) has two undesirable problems:
It does not preserve all infinite (say countable) sums of
C^{*}
-algebras, and it does not send every exact sequence
of
C^{*}
-algebras to a fibre sequence. One can improve on both by modifying the construction of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.
If one just replaces semiexactness by exactness, then one obntains the equivariant
E
-theory functor

\mathrm{ee}^{G}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}^{G}
which we will not consider further in the present paper, but see
[
Bun23
]
and the references therein. If one further forces the preservation of countable sums, then
we get the countable sum-preserving version of equivariant
E
-theory

\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G}\ .

(2.20)

In the following we explain some details of this functors and how it compares with the
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
-functor considered above.

The sum-preserving equivariant
E
-theory functor (
2.20
)
has a similar characterization as
KK
-theory functor in (
2.2
):
Pull-back along
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G,*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G},{\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{sfin},\oplus}(GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathcal{C}})

(2.21)

for every cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, were:

1.

\mathrm{ex}
stands for exactness and means that
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}
send all exact sequences of
C^{*}
-algebras to fibre sequences.

2.

\oplus
stands for the condition that the natural map

\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}(A_{i})\to\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}(\bigoplus_{i\in I}A_{i})

is an equivalence for every
countable family
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

The other conditions are the same as explained after (
2.20
).
The construction of the functor (
2.20
) is analogous to the construction
of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
and involves Dwyer-Kan-localizations and
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-completions in order to enforce the desired universal property. Since the maximal tensor product, the induction, restriction and maximal crossed product functors preserve
countable sums and exact sequences they all descend to
\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\oplus}
. In particular, in analogy of (
2.3
), the pull-back along the symmetric monoidal refinement of
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G}
induces an equivalence

\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}^{G,*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G},{\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\otimes/\mathrm{lax}}^{h,K_{G},\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{sfin},\oplus}(GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathcal{C}})

for any presentably symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
.
In the case of
E
-theory we should disregard all statements involving minimal tensor products or reduced crossed products in cases where they do not coincide already with their maximal counterparts e.g. because a tensor factor is nuclear (or belongs to the UCT class), or the group is amenable.
An additional nice feature of the functor in (
2.20
) is that it preserves all countable filtered colimits (see the text before
[
MN06
, Lem. 2.7]
).
6
6

6

In a forthcoming paper we will show that for a discrete group
G
the category
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G}
is a dualizable object in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
. This has further
interesting consequences e.g. for the phantom maps discussed in
Section

2.3
below.

For the following we equip
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
with the maximal tensor product structure.
The universal property (
2.3
) provides a
canonical left-adjoint symmetric monoidal functor

c^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G}\ .

(2.22)

It equips
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}^{G}
with the structure of a commutative algebra over
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
.
The functor (
2.22
) is a Dwyer-Kan localization forcing exactness and preservation of
countable sums. Therefore all the adjunctions and equivalences between functors in
KK
-theory
descend to corresponding adjunctions between their descends to sum-preserving
E
-theory.

The
K
-theory functor
K:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
it known to be an object of

{\mathbf{Fun}}_{\mathrm{lax}}^{h,K,\mathrm{ex},\mathrm{sfin},\oplus}(C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}},{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU))\ .

By the universal property
(
2.21
) it has an essentially unique factorization

K:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}}\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{K}^{E}}{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)

(2.23)

through a lax symmetric monoidal colimit preserving functor
\mathrm{K}^{E}
.
One can then show that

\mathrm{K}^{E}\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}},-)
,
and that
\mathrm{K}\simeq\mathrm{K}^{E}\circ c
.

Proposition 2.7

(
1.6
)
.

Assume that
G
is discrete. If
A
is in
\langle{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{KK})\rangle
, then we have an equivalence

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(A))\simeq\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}G)\ .

(2.24)

Proof.

The idea of the proof is to define a natural transformation
(
2.32
) between colimit preserving functors on
\mathrm{KK}^{G}

and to show that it induces an equivalence on the image of the induction functor
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
.
We have already seen by an explicit calculation in
1.3
that on induced algebras both sides of this transformation are equivalent. The main technical problem that we will solve in this proof
is to construct the transformation in such a way that one can see that it induces the above equivalence.

We first describe the construction assembly map (
2.28
) between
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
-valued functors. For any
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
A
we have a natural isomorphism in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

A\otimes C_{0}(G)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(A))\otimes C_{0}(G)\ .

Identifying elements of the tensor product with functions
f:G\to A
this isomorphism is given by

f\mapsto(g\mapsto g^{-1}f(g))
. Under this isomorphism the
G
-action by right-translations on the
C_{0}(G)
-factor on the left goes to the action
(h,f)\mapsto(g\mapsto hf(gh))
on the right.
On the level of functors from
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
this isomorphism induces an equivalence

\mathrm{triv}^{G}(-)\otimes C_{0}(G)\simeq\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-))\otimes C_{0}(G)\ ,

(2.25)

where
\mathrm{triv}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
equips an object with the trivial
G
-action.
We now have the following chain of equivalences of functors from
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
:

\displaystyle(\mathrm{triv}^{G}(-)\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G)))\rtimes_{r}G

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{bfsbspokvopsrwgfergvfrrrs}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-))\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G)))\rtimes_{r}G

(2.26)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{fwerfeiuhieuworf}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-)\otimes(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\rtimes_{r}G)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qewfiojioefqwdwedewqdedqd}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-)\otimes\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-) .

We have a map
*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}\to C_{0}(G)
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
,
where we consider the free
G
-indexed product
*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}
of copies of
{\mathbb{C}}
as a
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra for the left action on the index set with the additional
action by right translations. The map is fixed such that

{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{g}}*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}\to C_{0}(G)
sends
1
to
\delta_{g}
for every
g
in
G
.
The map
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(G))
is an equivalence in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
. This follows e.g. from
[
BELb
, Prop. 6.9]

applied to the
G
-
C^{*}
-category
{\mathbb{C}}[G]
obtained by linearizing the discrete
G
-category
G
.
We therefore have an equivalence

\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{kofpqfdewdfeqwfeqwfed}}}{{\simeq}}(\mathrm{triv}^{G}(-)\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\rtimes_{r}G\ ,

(2.27)

of functors from
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
.
We have a morphism
\sigma:*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
which is fixed such that

{\mathbb{C}}\xrightarrow{\iota_{g}}*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
is the identity for all
g
in
G
. It induces a morphism

\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}})
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
and hence, using some obvious identifications, a natural transformation

\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{vsdfvoijdfvovjiosdfvfdv}}}{{\simeq}}(\mathrm{triv}^{G}(-)\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\rtimes_{r}G\to\mathrm{triv}^{G}(-\rtimes_{r}G)

of functors from
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
.
Its adjoint is a natural transformation

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-)\to(-\rtimes_{r}G):\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to\mathrm{KK}\ .

(2.28)

We must show that it induces an equivalence after precomposition with
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
.
Equivalently, we must show that the adjoint of

(\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\rtimes_{r}G\to\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A)\rtimes_{r}G)

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{KK}
.
Since
-\rtimes_{r}
preserves colimits it suffices to show that the adjoint of

\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})\to\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))

(2.29)

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

For
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the induction of
A
is the
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A)\cong C_{0}(G,A)

with the
G
-action
(g,f)\mapsto(h\mapsto f(g^{-1}h))
.
We have a natural isomorphism

\displaystyle\mathrm{triv}^{G}(C_{0}(G,A))\otimes*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle C_{0}(G,A\otimes*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})

(2.30)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f\mapsto\tilde{f}}}{{\cong}}

\displaystyle C_{0}(G,A\otimes*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathrm{triv}^{G}(C_{0}(G,A))\otimes\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.
Here
\tilde{f}(g):=g^{-1}f(g)
for all
g
in
G
.
The first copy of
C_{0}(G,A\otimes*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})
is a
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
with the action
(g,f)\mapsto(h\mapsto gf(g^{-1}h))
and has the additional
G
-action
(l,f)\mapsto(h\mapsto f(gl))
. The second copy
is a
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
with the action
(g,f)\mapsto(h\mapsto f(g^{-1}h))
and has the additional
G
-action
(l,f)\mapsto(h\mapsto f(gl))
. One checks that

\textstyle{\mathrm{triv}^{G}(C_{0}(G,A))\otimes*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
2.30
)
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{triv}^{G}(C_{0}(G,A))\otimes\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{triv}^{G}(C_{0}(G,A))}

commutes, where the diagonal arrows are both induced by
\sigma
.
Applying
\mathrm{kk}^{G}
we get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
2.29
)
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))\otimes\mathrm{Res}_{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}})))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))}

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK}^{G})
for any
A
in
\mathrm{KK}
.
We must show that the adjoint of the right diagonal arrow is an equivalence.
Since
\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A))\otimes\mathrm{Res}_{G}(-)
preserves colimits it suffices to show that the adjoint of

\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\to\mathrm{triv}^{G}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})

(2.31)

is an equivalence in
\mathrm{KK}
.
We have an equivalence

\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\simeq\mathrm{kk}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(*_{G}{\mathbb{C}}))\simeq\mathrm{kk}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(C_{0}(G))\simeq\bigoplus_{G}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}

in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,\mathrm{KK})
, where
G
acts on the index set of the sum by right translations.
Under this identification the map
in (
2.31
) goes to the canonical map which induces the identity on each summand.
The adjoint of this map is well-known to be an equivalence.

Applying the colimit preserving functor
\mathrm{K}:\mathrm{KK}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
to the assembly map (
2.28
)
we get the assembly map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BG}\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(-))\to\mathrm{K}(-\rtimes_{r}G):\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)

(2.32)

appearing in the statement of
1.6
.
Above we have shown that
(
2.32
) is an equivalence on the image of
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.
Since it is a natural transformation between colimit preserving functors
it is then also an equivalence on
\langle{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{KK})\rangle
. ∎

Remark 2.8
.

For torsion-free groups the equivalence asserted in
1.6
can be considered as a special case of a result of
[
OO97
]
or
[
CE01
]
after identifying the left-hand side with the usual left-hand side of the Baum-Connes assembly map as in (
1.6
).

The proof for general discrete groups could be also deduced from
the results of
[
Kra20
]
or
[
BELa
]
. One only needs to observe
that the functor

K^{G}_{-}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{Orb},{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU))

mentioned in
1.2

factorizes over a colimit preserving functor

\mathcal{K}_{-}^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\mathbf{Orb},{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU))

which has the following properties:

1.

\mathcal{K}^{G}_{A}(G/H)\simeq A\rtimes_{r}H
for all subgroups
H
of
G
.

2.

\mathcal{K}^{G}_{A}(G)\simeq\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(A))
, where
G
acts on the left-hand side by right-translations on the
G
-orbit
G
.

3.

For
B
in
\mathrm{KK}
the functor
\mathcal{K}_{{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(B)}^{G}
is the left Kan-extension along the inclusion
BG\to G\mathbf{Orb}
of
the constant functor on
BG
with value
\mathrm{K}(B)
.

The existence of such a factorization has been shown in
[
Kra20
]
, see also
[
BELa
, Sec. 16]
.
Using these properties it is a simple exercise with Kan extension to deduce

1.6
.

The main point of the proof given above
is to bypass the construction of the functor
\mathcal{K}^{G}_{-}
and in particular the verification of the Property
3
, which in the references was quite involved.
See
3.16
for an analogous result for finite groups
G
.
Note that most of the argument for
1.6
above
is devoted to the verification of an analogous result.

∎

Let
A
be an object of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
and
\rho
in
\pi_{0}R(G)
denote the class of the left-regular representation of the finite group
G
.

Proposition 2.9

(
1.8
)
.

We assume that
G
is finite. The following assertions are equivalent.

1.

\rho
acts as an equivalence on
A
.

2.

We have
A\in\langle{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{KK})\rangle
and
|G|
acts an equivalence on
A
.

Proof.

We first show that
1
implies
2
.
Since
G\times G
decomposes as a
G
-set into a union of
|G|
copies of
G
we have the relation
\rho^{2}\simeq|G|\rho
in
\pi_{0}R(G)
. So if
\rho
acts as an equivalence on
A
, then so does
|G|
.
We now use
2.6
for
H=\{e\}
.
The composition (
2.18
) for
f={\mathtt{id}}_{A}

provides a factorization of the multiplication map by
\rho
through

A\to{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(A))\to A\ .

Since
\rho
acts as an equivalence on
A
we conclude that
A
is a retract of
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(A))
.

We now argue that
2
implies
1
.
Since the fact that
\rho
acts as an equivalence is preserved under colimits it suffices to show that
\rho
acts as an equivalence on
A:={\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(B)
for any
B
in
\mathrm{KK}
such that
|G|
acts as an equivalence on
A
.
By the projection formula (
2.11
) and using
\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\rho)=|G|
we get a chain of equivalences of morphisms

\displaystyle(\rho:A\to A)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle(\rho:{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(B)\to{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(B))

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{G}(\rho):B\to B)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle({\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(|G|:B\to B))

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle(|G|:A\to A)

We can conclude that
\rho:A\to A
is an equivalence.
∎

2.2
Power functors

In the last subsection
Section

2.1
we described exact restriction and induction functors which can be used to describe objects in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
. Since these functors fit into adjunctions we can understand the resulting objects well. In the present subsection we introduce non-exact multiplicative induction functors. They produce
objects of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
which do not have an obvious presentation in terms of exact constructions.

Remark 2.10
.

The multiplicative induction functors are part of a global symmetric monoidal structure on the global category
\underline{\mathrm{KK}}
(see also
2.13
below) which will be discussed in future work. The present subsection is designed to provide a reference for all technical background results for this project which require to go through the
C^{*}
-algebraic details of the construction of the stable
\infty
-categories
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
. For this reason it is slightly more general than need for the purpose of the present paper.

∎

In order encode the naturality properties of the multiplicative induction functors properly we will work with finite groupoids. A finite groupoid
{\mathcal{G}}
is connected of every two objects are connected by a morphisms. Assume that
{\mathcal{G}}
is a connected finite groupoid. If we fix
an object
x_{0}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
and let
G
denote its stabilizer group, then the inclusion
BG\to{\mathcal{G}}
is an equivalence of groupoids, where we consider
BG
as a groupoid with a single object.

Example 2.11
.

Let
S
be a finite
G
-set. Then we can form the action groupoid
S\curvearrowleft G
. It is connected if and only if
S
is transitive. All finite connected groupoids are of this form for some group
G
and transitive
G
set
S
.
∎

Remark 2.12
.

An object
A
in
{\mathcal{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:={\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{G}},C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is
a collection
(A_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}
of
C^{*}
-algebras together with homomorphisms

g:A_{x}\to A_{x^{\prime}}
for all
g:x\to x^{\prime}
satisfying the associativity rule.
A morphism
A\to B
is a family of morphisms

(f_{x}:A_{x}\to B_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}
which is compatible with the morphisms of
{\mathcal{G}}
.

If
{\mathcal{G}}
is connected, then the
restriction along the equivalence of groupoids
BG\to{\mathcal{G}}
induces an equivalence of categories
{\mathcal{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. In particular,
every object of
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{G}},C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is determined up to unique isomorphism by
the object
A_{x_{0}}
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, and
a morphism is uniquely determined by
a
G
-equivariant homomorphism
f_{x_{0}}:A_{x_{0}}\to B_{x_{0}}
.

∎

The construction of
\mathrm{kk}^{G}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
from
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
can be adjusted to the construction of a functor

\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{G}}}:{\mathcal{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}

such that restriction along
\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{G}}}

has the universal property (
2.2
) with
G
replaced by
{\mathcal{G}}
.

Generalizing the restriction functors
\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{H}
from (
2.5
), a morphism of finite connected groupoids
{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
induces a restriction functor

\mathrm{Res}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}:\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{H}}}
.
In particular, the restriction

\mathrm{Res}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{BG}:\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{BG}\simeq\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is an equivalence, where the right term
just denote the
G
-equivariant
\mathrm{KK}
-category in the usual notation.

Remark 2.13
.

The association
{\mathcal{G}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}
is part of the structure of a global category
\underline{\mathrm{KK}}
, see e.g.

[
CLL23
, Ex, 5.10]
.
∎

Example 2.14
.

Let
G
be a finite group and
f:S\to T
be an equivariant map of transitive
G
-sets. In induces a map of finite connected
groupoids
S\curvearrowleft G\to T\curvearrowleft G
which has the special property of being a faithful isofibration, i.e., for every morphism
g:t\to t^{\prime}
in
T\curvearrowleft G
and
s
in
f^{-1}(t)
there exists a unique morphism
s\to s^{\prime}
with
f(s\to s^{\prime})=(t\to t^{\prime})
. It is implemented by the same group element
g
.
∎

From now one we consider either the minimal or maximal tensor products of
C^{*}
-algebras.
Let
\phi:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
be a faithful isofibration.

Definition 2.15
.

We define the multiplicative induction functor

{\mathtt{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\otimes,{\mathcal{G}}}:{\mathcal{H}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to{\mathcal{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

as follows:

1.

objects: It sends
(A_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{H}})}
to
(\bigotimes_{x\in\phi^{-1}(\{y\})}A_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}
. To a morphism

g:y\to y^{\prime}
we associate the morphism

(\otimes_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}g_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}:(\bigotimes_{x\in\phi^{-1}(\{y\})}A_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}\to(\bigotimes_{x^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(\{y^{\prime}\})}A_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}\ ,

where
g_{x}:x\to x^{\prime}
is the unique lift of
g:y\to y^{\prime}
with domain
x
.

2.

morphisms: The functor sends a morphism
(f_{x}:(A_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{H}})}\to(B_{x})_{x\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{H}})})
in

{\mathcal{H}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to the morphism

(\otimes_{x\in\phi^{-1}(\{y\})}f_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}:(\bigotimes_{x\in\phi^{-1}(\{y\})}A_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}\to(\bigotimes_{x^{\prime}\in\phi^{-1}(\{y^{\prime}\})}B_{x})_{y\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})}\ ,

Example 2.16
.

The most important example is the map
G\curvearrowleft G\to*\curvearrowleft G\simeq BG
. We get the power functor

(-)^{\otimes G}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\simeq{\mathbf{Fun}}(G\curvearrowleft G,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{Ind}}_{G\curvearrowleft G}^{*\curvearrowleft G}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(*\curvearrowleft G,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\simeq GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

It sends a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
to the
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
A^{\otimes G}:=\bigotimes_{g\in G}A

with the
G
-action on the index set.
∎

The main result of the present subsection is the verification that the multiplicative induction functors descend to
KK
-theory.

Theorem 2.17
.

There exists an essentially unique factorization

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 138.54369pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Proof.

In a first step we reduce the problem to the more common case of groups.
We fix a point
y_{0}
in
\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{G}})
and let
G
denote the stabilizer. Then there exists
x_{0}\in\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{H}})
such that
f(x_{0})=y_{0}
, and we let
H
denote the stabilizer of
x_{0}
. In view of the cube

\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{H}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\otimes,{\mathcal{G}}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Res}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{BH}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathcal{H}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{H}}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{Ind}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}^{\otimes,{\mathcal{G}}}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{H}}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{\otimes G/H}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\mathcal{G}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{G}}}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Res}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{BG}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}

it suffices to construct
(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}}
on the right.

The explicit description of the symmetric monoidal functor

(-)^{\otimes G/H}:HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

depends on the choice of a functor
{\mathcal{H}}\to BH

and a natural isomorphism between
{\mathcal{H}}\to BH\to{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathcal{H}}}
. For both we choose a collection
of morphisms
h_{x}:x\to x_{0}
for all
x
in
\mathrm{Ob}({\mathcal{H}})
. The functor

{\mathcal{H}}\to BH
is given on morphisms by
(h:x\to x^{\prime})\mapsto h_{x^{\prime}}hh_{x}^{-1}
, and the components of the transformation are
h_{x}^{-1}:x_{0}\to x
. The functor
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
sends a
H
-
C^{*}
-algebra
A
to

A^{\otimes G/H}:=\bigotimes_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{0})}A\ .

The action of
g
in
G
on this tensor product ist then given by

\otimes_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{0})}h_{gx}g_{x}h_{x}^{-1}:\bigotimes_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{0})}A\to\bigotimes_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{0})}A\ .

(2.33)

Note that
h_{gx}g_{x}h_{x}^{-1}\in H
for all
g
in
G
and
x
in
f^{-1}(y_{0})
.

Remark 2.18
.

Our main reason to work with groupoids instead of groups is that the multiplicative
induction functor in
2.15
is given by a completely canonical formula. In contrast, the formula (
2.33
) for the power functor
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
depends on the choices made in the construction of an inverse
of the inclusion
BH\to{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

We first restrict to separable
C^{*}
-algebras and indicate this by a subscript
\mathrm{sep}
.
Recall from
[
BELb
, Def. 4.1]
that a functor
C
between categories of
C^{*}
-algebras with actions of groups is
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-
s
-finitary if for every
A
the inductive system
(C(A^{\prime})^{C(A)})_{A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A}

(see
Section

2.1
.
4
for notation) of images of
C(A^{\prime})\to C(A)
is cofinal in
B\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}C(A)
,
and the canonical map
(C(A^{\prime}))_{A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A}\to(C(A^{\prime})^{C(A)})_{A^{\prime}\subseteq_{\mathrm{sep}}A}
is an isomorphism of inductive systems.

Proposition 2.19
.

There exists an essentially unique symmetric monoidal factorization

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 52.4319pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.20609pt\raise 7.20335pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.23114pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 132.03322pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 132.03322pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 63.6346pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 105.20815pt\raise-24.20392pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99225pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 132.03322pt\raise-7.9748pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 145.63118pt\raise-32.85672pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

In addition
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
is
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-
s
-finitary

Proof.

The proof of the second statement is analogous to
the proof of
[
BELb
, Prop. 3.8]
. We now concentrate on the first assertion.
Since
\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is a Dwyer-Kan localization at the
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{H}
-equivalences by
[
BELb
, Def. 2.1]
it suffices to show that
the composition
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}^{G}\circ(-)^{\otimes G/H}
sends
\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalences to equivalences.

We will use the construction of the functor
\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}:HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
as a sequence of localizations (see
[
Bun23
]
for the non-equivariant case, the equivariant case is completely analogous).

\hskip-28.45274pt\mathrm{kk}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}:HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{L_{h}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\xrightarrow{L_{K_{H}}}L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}\xrightarrow{L_{\mathrm{se}}}L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}\xrightarrow{L_{\mathrm{group}}}\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ .

1.

L_{h}
presents the symmetric monoidal left-exact
\infty
-category
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
as the symmetric monoidal Dwyer-Kan localization of
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
at the homotopy equivalences.
The mapping spaces in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
are represented by the topological mapping spaces in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}

given by the
{\mathtt{Hom}}
-sets with the point norm topology.
The localization
L_{h}
preserves products, coproducts and sends exact sequences
0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{\pi}C\to 0

in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
with
\pi
a Schochet fibration
[
Sch82
]
(i.e.,
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}}(D,\pi)
is a Serre fibration for every
D
in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
) to fibre sequences
L_{h}(A)\to L_{h}(B)\to L_{h}(C)
.

2.

L_{K_{H}}
is the symmetric monoidal Bousfield localization of
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
at the full subcategory the
K_{H}
-stable algebras.
The left-exact localization functor
L_{K_{H}}
sends
A
to
A\otimes K_{H}
, where
K_{H}:=K(L^{2}(H)\otimes\ell^{2})
. It presents the pre-additive
\infty
-category
L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
as the Dwyer-Kan localization of
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
at the left-upper corner inclusion

A\to A\otimes K_{H}
for all
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
using the projection
p
in
K_{H}
projecting on the subspace of
L^{2}(H)\otimes\ell^{2}
generated by
{\mathtt{const}}_{1}\otimes e_{0}
.

3.

For any semisplit exact sequence
0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{\pi}C\to 0
(i.e.,
\pi
admits an equivariant completely positive contractive right-inverse) in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

we can consider the map
\iota_{\pi}:A\to C(\pi)
(see Remark
2.20
below for notation) representing the map from
A
into the homotopy fibre of
\pi
. The left-exact functor
L_{\mathrm{se}}
presents the symmetric monoidal left-exact
\infty
-category

L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
as
the symmetric monoidal Dwyer-Kan localization at the closure under
2
-out-of-
3
and pull-backs of the set of these morphisms
\iota_{\pi}
for all semisplit exact sequences. The functor
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
sends
exact sequences
0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{\pi}C\to 0
with
\pi
a Schochet fibration or an equivariant completely positive contractive split
to fibre sequences.

4.

L_{\mathrm{group}}
is the symmetric monoidal right Bousfield localization of
L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
at the full subcategory of group objects. The left-exact localization functor
L_{\mathrm{group}}
sends
A
to its two-fold suspension
S^{2}(A)
.
We have a natural transformation
\beta:S^{2}\to{\mathtt{id}}
of endofunctors of

L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
, and
L_{\mathrm{group}}
presents
\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
as the Dwyer-Kan localization of
L_{K_{H}}HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
at the
components of this transformation.

Remark 2.20
.

If
B\xrightarrow{\pi}C
is a map of
C^{*}
-algebras, then we form
the mapping zylinder

Z(\pi):=\{(f,b)\in C([0,1],C)\oplus B\mid f(0)=\pi(b)\}\ .

It comes with a Schochet fibration
\hat{\pi}:Z(\pi)\to C
given by
(f,b)\mapsto f(1)
and a homotopy equivalence
h:B\to Z(\pi)
given by
b\mapsto({\mathtt{const}}_{\pi(b)},b)
. The kernel

C(\pi):=\{(f,b)\in Z(\pi)\mid f(1)=0\}

of
\hat{\pi}
represents the homotopy fibre of
\pi
and we have a map
\iota_{A}:A\to C(\pi)
given by
a\mapsto(0,a)
, where
A:={\mathtt{ker}}(\pi)
.

In order to see these statements note that the Schochet fibrations and homotopy equivalences are part of a fibration category structure on
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
which models the localization
L_{h}
, see
[
Uuy13
]
for the non-equivariant case and
[
BELb
, Prop. 2.10]
for the equivariant case (for discrete groups).

∎

We now consider the following diagram

62.94733pt\raise-103.57668pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99225pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(-)_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}^{\otimes G/(H}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 130.868pt\raise-70.86816pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise We will obtain the four symmetric monoidal factorizations from the universal properties of the localizations at the left.

1.

The functor
(-)^{\otimes G/H}:HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
defined above is continuous and hence preserves homotopy equivalences.
Since
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
is homotopy invariant, also the composition
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}\circ(-)^{\otimes G/H}:GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

is homotopy invariant. We get the factorization
F_{1}
from the universal property of
L_{h}
.

2.

We consider
u:{\mathbb{C}}\to K_{H}
induced by the projection
p
described in
Item

2
.
For
A
in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h}
we get the left upper corner embedding
{\mathtt{id}}_{A}\otimes u:A\to A\otimes K_{H}
.
We must show that the induced map
F_{1}({\mathtt{id}}_{A}\otimes u):F_{1}(A)\to F_{1}(A\otimes K)
is an equivalence.
Since
F_{1}
is symmetric monoidal we have an equivalence
F_{1}({\mathtt{id}}_{A}\otimes u)\simeq{\mathtt{id}}_{F_{1}(A)}\otimes F_{1}(u)
and it suffices to show that
F_{1}(u)
is an equivalence.

We have an isomorphism
K_{H}^{\otimes G/H}\cong\bigotimes_{x\in f^{-1}(y_{0})}K_{H}
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
under which the
homomorphism
u^{\otimes G/H}
is induced by the
G
-invariant projection
\otimes_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y_{0}\})}p
. Since the latter is a one-dimensional we can conclude from the
K_{G}
-stability of

\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(u^{\otimes G/H})
is an equivalence.
Hence we get the factorization
F_{2}
from the universal property of
L_{K_{H}}
.

3.

Let
0\to A\to B\xrightarrow{\pi}C\to 0
be a semisplit exact sequence in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. We then form the diagram

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{\pi}}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z(\pi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\pi}}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

in
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
,
where
h
is a homotopy equivalence. Since the target of
F_{2}
is stable and
F_{2}
is left-exact
it suffices (i.e. we do not have to discuss the closure of the set of these maps under pull-back and
2
-out-of-
3
) to show that
F_{2}(\iota_{\pi})
is an equivalence for all exact sequences as above.
Equivalently, we must show that the induced map

\iota_{\pi}^{\otimes G/H}:A^{\otimes G/H}\to C(\pi)^{\otimes G/H}

is a
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence.

For
m
in
\{0,\dots,|G/H|\}
we let
I_{m}
be the
G
-invariant ideal in
B^{\otimes G/H}
generated by elementary tensors which have at least
m
factors in
A
. The family
(I_{m})_{m=0,\dots,|G/H|}
is a decreasing filtration
of
B^{\otimes G/H}
by
G
-invariant ideals such that
I_{|G/H|}\cong A^{\otimes G/H}
,

I_{1}={\mathtt{ker}}(B^{\otimes G/H}\to C^{\otimes G/H})
and
I_{0}=B^{\otimes G/H}
.
We introduce the abbreviation
Z:=f^{-1}(\{y_{0}\})
for the finite
G
-set given by the fibre of
f
over
y_{0}
. Then
I_{m}\cong\sum_{F\in\mathcal{P}_{m}(Z)}A^{\otimes F}\otimes B^{\otimes Z\setminus F}
and
I_{m}/I_{m+1}\cong\bigoplus_{F\in\mathcal{P}_{m}(Z)}A^{\otimes F}\otimes C^{\otimes Z\setminus F}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{m}(Z):=\{F\in\mathcal{P}(Z)\mid|F|=m\}
is the set of subsets of
Z
with
m
elements. The sequence

0\to I_{m+1}\to I_{m}\to I_{m}/I_{m+1}\to 0

(2.34)

has an induced equivariant semisplit given by tensor products of the semisplits
C\to B
.
We write
(J_{m})_{m=0,\dots,|Z|}
for the corresponding sequence of ideals for the sequence
0\to C(\pi)\to Z(\pi)\to C\to 0
. We then have a map

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I_{m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{m+1}}
\textstyle{I_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{I_{m}/I_{m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q_{m}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{m}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J_{m}/J_{m+1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

between semisplit exact sequences in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
which induces a map of fibre sequences
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
upon application of
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
. We want to conclude by an induction by the order of
G
, the number of elements of
Z
and
m
that the maps
\iota_{m}
are
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalences.
We start with
m=0
. Then
h_{0}=h^{\otimes Z}
is a homotopy equivalence and
q_{0}={\mathtt{id}}_{C^{\otimes Z}}
is an isomorphism. Therefore
\iota_{1}
is a
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence.
For the induction step from
m
to
m+1
we assume that
m<|Z|
. We observe that

q_{m}\cong\bigoplus_{[F]\in\mathcal{P}_{m}(Z)/G}{\mathtt{Ind}}_{\mathrm{sep},G_{F}}^{G}(\iota_{\pi}^{\otimes F}\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{C}^{\otimes Z\setminus F})\ ,

(2.35)

where
G_{F}
denotes the stabilizer of
F
in
G
.
Note
\iota_{\pi}^{\otimes F}
is a
\mathrm{kk}^{G_{F}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence by our induction hypothesis.
It follows that
{\mathtt{Ind}}_{\mathrm{sep},G_{F}}^{G}(\iota_{\pi}^{\otimes F}\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{C}^{\otimes Z\setminus F})
is a
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence and we can conclude that
\iota_{m+1}
is a
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence.
Since
\iota_{|Z|}=\iota_{\pi}^{\otimes Z}=\iota^{\otimes G/H}
this finishes the argument.

4.

We must show that
F_{3}(\beta_{A})
is an equivalence in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
for every
A
in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Note that
C_{0}({\mathbb{C}})^{\otimes G/H}\cong C_{0}({\mathbb{C}}^{Z})
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, where the
G
-action on the right-hand side is implemented by an action on
{\mathbb{C}}^{Z}
by
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear automorphisms.
It follows that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}(C_{0}({\mathbb{C}}^{Z}))
is a tensor-invertible object of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
.
Since
F_{3}
is symmetric monoidal we have an equivalence

F_{3}(\beta_{A}\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}({\mathbb{C}})})\simeq F_{3}(\beta_{A})\otimes\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}({\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}({\mathbb{C}}^{Z})})
.
Hence it suffices to show that
F_{3}(\beta_{A}\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}({\mathbb{C}})})
is an equivalence.
But since

\beta_{A}\otimes{\mathtt{id}}_{C_{0}({\mathbb{C}})}\simeq\beta_{A\otimes C_{0}({\mathbb{C}})}\simeq\beta_{S^{2}(A)}
and

S^{2}(A)
is a group object in
L_{K_{H}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},h,\mathrm{se}}
we can conclude that already
\beta_{S^{2}(A)}
is an equivalence.
We get the factorization
(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
from the universal property of
L_{\mathrm{group}}
.

∎

Proposition 2.21
.

There exists an essentially unique factorization

\textstyle{HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{H}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{kk}^{{\mathcal{G}}}\circ(-)^{\otimes G/H}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{{\mathcal{G}}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}}}

such that
(-)_{\mathrm{kk}}^{\otimes G/H}
preserves all filtered colimits and compact objects.
A addition

(-)_{\mathrm{kk}}^{\otimes G/H}
has a symmetric monoidal refinement.

Proof.

By definition
y^{H}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{H}
and
y^{G}:\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
present
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
and
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
as the
{\mathtt{Ind}}
-completions of
\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}
and
\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}
, and their images consist of the compact objects in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
or
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
, respectively. Since the desired functor

(-)_{\mathrm{kk}}^{\otimes G/H}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is required to preserve filtered colimits and compact objects it is necessarily the essentially unique such functor
such that

\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{H}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}}
\scriptstyle{y}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{y^{G}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{H}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-)_{\mathrm{kk}}^{\otimes G/H}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}

commutes.
The symmetric monoidal structure of
(-)^{\otimes Z}_{\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}}
induces a the symmetric monoidal refinement of

(-)_{\mathrm{kk}}^{\otimes Z}
.

We now consider the following diagram:

(2.36)

Our task is to show that the lower square commutes in an essentially unique way. We already know that all other squares commute. They provide a filler of the pentagon

The left composition from
HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}
to
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is clearly
s
-finitary, and
the right composition is
s
-finitary by the second assertion of
2.19
and
[
BELb
, Lem. 4.2]
.
Hence we get an essentially unique filler of the lower square whose composition with the obvious filler of the upper triangle is the filler of the pentagon. ∎

∎

From now on we will often write
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
also for
(-)^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{kk}}
.

Remark 2.22
.

In order to show the first assertion of
Theorem

2.17
in the case of a trivial group
H

one could use the characterization of
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\to\mathrm{KK}_{\mathrm{sep}}
as the universal functor to a stable
\infty
-category which sends
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalences to equivalences
[
LN18
]
,
[
BELb
, Def. 4.2]
.
Indeed, one can show that the composition

HC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{(-)^{\otimes G/H}}GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}}\mathrm{KK}^{G}_{\mathrm{sep}}

sends
\mathrm{kk}_{\mathrm{sep}}
-equivalence to equivalences
[
CEKN22
, Lem. 2.4]
.
The idea for the filtrations used in the step
3
above is taken from
[
CEKN22
]
.

∎

Let
B
be in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
and let
p
be a prime.

Lemma 2.23
.

If
p
acts as an equivalence on
B
, then it acts as an equivalence on
B^{\otimes G/H}
.

Proof.

Since
p
acts as an equivalence on
B
we have an equivalence

B\simeq B\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]
in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
. Since
the multiplicative induction functor
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
is symmetric monoidal we get an equivalence

B^{\otimes G/H}\simeq B^{\otimes G/H}\otimes{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]^{\otimes G/H}
.
It suffices to show that

p
acts as an equivalence on
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]^{\otimes G/H}
. We have

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}\left({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}\dots\right)\ .

Since
(-)^{\otimes G/H}
preserves filtered colimit we get

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]^{\otimes G/H}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}\left({\mathbf{1}}^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\otimes G/H}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\otimes G/H}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{\otimes G/H}}}{{\to}}\dots\right)\ .

We now use that
{\mathbf{1}}^{\otimes G/H}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}\simeq{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}
and that the map

p^{\otimes G/H}
is the action of
p^{|G/H|}
. Hence

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}[p^{-1}]^{\otimes G/H}\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}\left({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{|G/H|}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{|G/H|}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p^{|G/H|}}}{{\to}}\dots\right)\simeq{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{G}}[p^{-1}]\ .

∎

2.3
Phantoms

One application of homotopy theory in the present paper is to
extend the validity of a statement about
G
-
C^{*}
-algebras from a small
initial set of examples to a much bigger set. For example,
if the statement is that a natural transformation between functors is an equivalence, and the functors have colimit preserving factorizations over
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
, then we can obviously extend to the colimit closure of the set of examples in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

This idea has been applied in the proof of
1.6

where the initial set of examples was the image of the induction functor
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
.
As we shall see in
Section

2.4
the Rokhlin property for
A
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
does not imply that
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A)
belongs to the colimit closure
of the image of
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
itself, but it does imply a slightly weaker statement, namely being in the closure of the the image of
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
under summable phantom retracts. In order to formalizes this
and eventually show
1.4
, in the present section we recall some general
\infty
-categorical notions. The language developed here can be used in general to capture asymptotic properties
of
C^{*}
-algebras and maps homotopically. We will indicate the main idea in
2.39
.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.24
.

A morphism
C\to D
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is called compact
7
7

7

We took this definition from a lecture series of Dustin Clausen at IHES, 2023 on Efimov’s K-theory, where he attributed it to Akhil Mathew. Compact morphisms play an important role in the intrinsic characterization of dualizable objects in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
.

if for any filtered diagram
Z:I\to{\mathbf{C}}

with
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I}Z\simeq 0
and morphism
D\to Z(i)
for some
i
in
I
there exists a morphism
i\to j
in
I
such that the composition
C\to D\to Z(i)\to Z(j)
vanishes.

Remark 2.25
.

It might be useful to record the following equivalent characterization of the compactness of a morphism
C\to D
:
For every filtered diagram
Z:I\to{\mathbf{C}}
and map
D\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I}Z
there exists an
i
in
I
and a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 7.27847pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.93124pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.2919pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.33334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 41.2919pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Z(i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.6742pt\raise-24.33334pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-7.27847pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{D\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.27847pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 31.27847pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I}Z}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(2.37)

∎

Note that an object
C
is compact if
{\mathtt{id}}_{C}:C\to C
is a compact morphism.
If
C
or
D
is a compact object, then every morphism
C\to D
is compact.

Definition 2.26
.

1.

A morphism
D\to E
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is called a strong phantom map if the composition
C\to D\to E
vanishes for
every compact map
C\to D
.

2.

An object
D
is called a strong phantom object if
{\mathtt{id}}_{D}
is a strong phantom map.

Remark 2.27
.

Classically a morphism
D\to E
is called a phantom map, if for every morphism
C\to D
from a compact object
C

the composition
C\to D\to E
vanishes. An object
D
is called phantom if
{\mathtt{id}}_{D}
is phantom.
A strong phantom morphism is a phantom morphism, and a strong phantom object is a phantom object.
If
{\mathbf{C}}
is compactly generated, then the notions of a strong phantom morphism and phantom morphism are actually equivalent. Moreover, zero is the only phantom object.

In the other extreme, if
0
is the only compact object of
{\mathbf{C}}
, then every morphism or object is phantom, but not necessarily strong phantom. So the strong versions of phantom objects and morphisms are interesting replacements of the classical notions in the case of cocomplete stable
\infty
-categories which are not compactly generated.
∎

We consider an adjunction

L:{\mathbf{C}}\leftrightarrows{\mathbf{D}}:R

between cocomplete stable
\infty
-categories.

Lemma 2.28
.

If
R
preserves colimits, then it preserves strong phantom morphisms and
L
preserves compact morphisms.

In our applications we encounter colimit preserving functors which are not right adjoints.
In order to control their interaction with the strong phantom morphisms we introduce the following stronger condition.

Definition 2.29
.

A morphism
C\to D
in
{\mathbf{C}}
is a universal (strong) phantom morphism, if
for every colimit preserving functor
F:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
the morphism
F(C)\to F(D)
is also
a (strong) phantom morphism.

We will actually encounter a potentially even stronger condition.

Definition 2.30
.

1.

A sequence
(f_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
of morphisms
f_{i}:C\to D
is called summable if
there exists a morphism
\hat{f}:C\to\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}D
with the components
(f_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
.

2.

A morphism
f:C\to D
is called a summable phantom map if the constant sequence
(f)_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
is summable.

In
1
we say that
\hat{f}
witnesses the summability of the sequence.

Remark 2.31
.

If
(f_{i})_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
is a summable sequence, then the choice of
\hat{f}
fixes a choice of a sum
\sum_{i\in{\rm Nature}}f_{i}:C\to D
, employing the universal property of
\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}
as the coproduct in
{\mathbf{C}}
. This motivates the word
summable
.
The sum may depend on the choice of
\hat{f}
.
∎

Remark 2.32
.

If the category
{\mathbf{C}}
is a dualizable presentable
\infty
-category, then one can show
that every strong phantom morphism between
\aleph_{1}
-compact objects is summable.
We will further observe that
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}
is dualizable. Note that

\mathrm{E}_{\oplus,\mathrm{sep}}
is precisely the subcategory of
\aleph_{1}
-compact
objects in
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}
. Therefore every strong phantom between objects in

\mathrm{E}_{\oplus,\mathrm{sep}}
(i.e.
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}
-classes of separable
C^{*}
-algebras) is summable.
We will discuss these facts in a future paper.
∎

Lemma 2.33
.

1.

A summable phantom is a universal strong phantom morphism.

2.

Countable colimit preserving functors preserve summable sequences.

Proof.

Let
f:C\to D
be a summable phantom morphism. We first show that
f
is a strong phantom morphism.
Let
F\to C
be a compact morphism. Then we can find (see
2.25
) an integer
k
and the dotted extension of the diagram

We then choose

bigger than

and get the dashed part of the diagram showing that the composition

vanishes. This shows that

is a strong phantom.

If
R
is a countable colimit preserving functor and
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
is a summable sequence of morphisms
f_{i}:C\to D
with summability witnessed by
\hat{f}
, then
R(\hat{f})
witnesses
(R(f_{i}))_{i\in{\rm Nature}}
as summable.
It then follows from the first part that a summable phantom map is actually a universal strong phantom map.
∎

Let
D
be an object of
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Definition 2.34
.

An object
C
is called a (summable, universal, strong) phantom retract of
D
if there exists
a map
C\to D
such that
{\mathrm{Fib}}(C\to D)\to C
is a (summable, universal, strong) phantom map.

A (strong) phantom object is a (strong) phantom retract of
0
.

Definition 2.35
.

If
{\mathbf{U}}
is a full subcategory of
{\mathbf{C}}
, then by
\overline{{\mathbf{U}}}
we denote the closure of
{\mathbf{U}}
under taking universal strong phantom retracts.

For any colimit preserving functor
R:{\mathbf{C}}\to{\mathbf{D}}
between cocomplete stable
\infty
-categories we have the inclusion

R(\overline{{\mathbf{U}}})\subseteq\overline{R({\mathbf{U}})}
, where
R({\mathbf{U}})
denotes the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{D}}
on the image of
R
.

Corollary 2.36
.

Assume that
{\mathbf{D}}
is compactly generated. If
R
is colimit preserving functor which annihilates
{\mathbf{U}}
, then
R
annihilates

\overline{{\mathbf{U}}}
.

Here is a general method to detect summable retracts.
We assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
admits countable products.

Lemma 2.37
.

If
C
fits into a diagram of the form

\textstyle{\prod_{{\rm Nature}}C}
\textstyle{C\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{Q}

(2.38)

with a horizontal fibre sequence,
then
C
is a summable phantom retract of
Q
.

Proof.

For any
n
in
{\rm Nature}
we can

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-46.83344pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 23.9591pt\raise-44.78691pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.93637pt\raise-45.9209pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p_{n}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.95828pt\raise-36.83331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.70752pt\raise-57.47913pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.65492pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 86.65492pt\raise-62.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{Q}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

(2.39)

where the lower diagonal dotted arrow is the composition
q\circ p
,
\iota
represents the fibre of this map,
and the dashed arrow is obtained from the universal property of the lower horizontal fibre sequence and the
vanishing of
F\xrightarrow{d\circ\iota}P\xrightarrow{q}Q
. The dashed arrow exhibits
\iota
as summable phantom.
∎

One can use the diagram (
2.38
) also to detect summable maps.

Corollary 2.38
.

If
f:D\to C
is a map such that
D\to C\xrightarrow{q\circ d}Q
vanishes, then

f
is a summable phantom map.

Proof.

The assumption implies that
f
has a factorization
D\to F\xrightarrow{\iota}C
.
∎

Example 2.39
.

Here we give a simple application of the language developed above.
Let
A,B
be
C^{*}
-algebras and
f,h:A\to B
be two morphisms.
One says that
f
and
h
are unitarily isomorphic if there exists a unitary
u
in
UM(B)
such that
h(-)=u^{*}f(-)u
. If
f
and
h
are unitarily isomorphic, then
\mathrm{kk}(f)\simeq\mathrm{kk}(h)
and therefore also
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(f)\simeq\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(h)
.

More generally, we say that
f
and
h
are approximately unitarily isomorphic
if there exists a sequence of unitaries
(u_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
in
UM(B)
such that
h(a)=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}u_{n}^{*}f(a)u_{n}
for every
a
in
A
.

Lemma 2.40
.

If
f
and
h
are approximately unitarily isomorphic, then
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(f)-\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(h)
is a summable phantom map in
\mathrm{E}_{\oplus}
.

Proof.

Let
(u_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
be a sequence in
UM(B)

exhibiting a unitary isomorphism between
f
and
h
.
We consider the exact sequence

0\to\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}B\to\prod_{{\rm Nature}}B\to B^{\infty}\to 0\ .

(2.40)

The sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
provides unitaries
u
in
UM(\prod_{{\rm Nature}}B)
and

\bar{u}
in
UM(B^{\infty})
.
We let

\bar{f}:A\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{diag}}}\prod_{{\rm Nature}}B\to B^{\infty}

and define
\bar{h}
similarly.
The assumptions imply that
\bar{f}(-)=\bar{u}^{*}h(-)\bar{u}
.

We form the diagram

\textstyle{\prod_{{\rm Nature}}\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(B)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathtt{diag}}}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{{\rm Nature}}\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{can}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(\prod_{{\rm Nature}}B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(B^{\infty})}

(2.41)

Since
\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}
preserves countable sums the lower sequence is a fibre sequence.
It is here we need to go from
KK
to sum-preserving
E
-theory.
Let
\delta:=\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(f)-\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(h)
.
Then

q\circ d\circ\delta\simeq\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(\bar{f})-\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(\bar{h}):\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(A)\to\mathrm{ee}_{\oplus}(B^{\infty})
vanishes since

\bar{f}
and
\bar{h}
are unitarily isomorphic.
By
2.38
we conclude that
\delta
is a summable phantom map.
∎

2.4
The Rokhlin property

The Rokhlin property of
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
A
a is usually defined
as an approximative condition. But using the techniques from
2.39
one can reformulate the condition as follows.

Definition 2.41

(
[
Izu04
, Def. 3.1]
)
.

A
has the Rokhlin property if there exists a family

(p_{g})_{g\in G}
of orthogonal projections in
M(A^{\infty})
such that:

1.

We have
\sum_{g\in G}p_{g}=1_{M(A^{\infty})}
.

2.

For for all
g,h
in
G
we have
hp_{g}=p_{hg}
.

3.

For every
g
in
G
and
a
in
A
we have

p_{g}a^{\prime}=a^{\prime}p_{g}
, where
a^{\prime}
denotes the image of
a
under and

A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{diag}}}}{{\to}}\prod_{{\rm Nature}}A\to A^{\infty}
.

Here
A^{\infty}
is defined as in (
2.40
).
The first two conditions say that
(p_{g})_{g\in G}
is an equivariant partition of unity.
Note that the definition as stated above is a natural extension of Izumi’s definition to non-unital algebras.

We let
A_{r}^{\infty}
denote the subalgebra of
A^{\infty}
generated by the products
p_{g}a^{\prime}
for all
g
in
G
and
a
in
A
. We note that the projections
p_{g}
belong to
M(A_{r}^{\infty})
and commute with all element of
A_{r}^{\infty}
.
We let
A_{r}^{\infty,G}
denote the subalgebra of
G
-fixed points in
A_{r}^{\infty}
.

Lemma 2.42
.

We have an isomorphism
A^{\infty}_{r}\cong{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A_{r}^{\infty,G})
in
GC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Proof.

We have an isomorphism of algebras
A_{r}^{\infty,G}\to p_{e}A_{r}^{\infty}
,
a\mapsto p_{e}a
with inverse

a\mapsto\sum_{g\in G}p_{g}ga
.
We identify
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(A_{r}^{\infty,G})\cong C(G,p_{e}A_{r}^{\infty})
and define a
G
-invariant isomorphism

C(G,p_{e}A_{r}^{\infty})\to A_{r}^{\infty}\ ,\quad f\mapsto\sum_{g\in G}p_{g}gf(g)\ .

Its inverse sends
a
to the function
g\mapsto p_{e}g^{-1}a
.
∎

The adjunction (
2.9
) descends through the comparison (
2.22
) between
KK
and sum-preserving
E
-theory and provides an adjunction

{\mathtt{Ind}}_{H}^{G}:\mathrm{E}^{H}_{\oplus}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{E}^{G}_{\oplus}:\mathrm{Res}_{H}^{G}\ .

We will use the case where
H
is the trivial group.
Recall that
\overline{{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{E}_{\oplus})}
denotes the closure of the image of
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}
under universal strong phantom retracts.

Proposition 2.43
.

If
A
has the Rokhlin property, then
\mathrm{ee}^{G}_{\oplus}(A)
belongs to
\overline{{\mathtt{Ind}}^{G}(\mathrm{E}_{\oplus})}
.

Proof.

We retain the notation from
2.42
.
We define the
G
-
C^{*}
-algebra
P
as the pull-back

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.98613pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.90451pt\raise It fits
into a commutative diagram
