by definition,
this implies that

\mathrm{K}^{G}(\hat{V}^{G}(E_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}G)\otimes A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}\mathbf{Orb}}\mathrm{K}^{G}(V^{G}\otimes A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{getgoijif0ewrfrwef}}}{{\simeq}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}\mathbf{Orb}}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\ .

∎

4.5
Reduction to cyclic subgroups

In this subsection we show
4.12
and
1.14
.

Lemma 4.12
.

If
G
is finite and not cyclic, then
\xi=0
.

Proof.

We must show that
\xi=0
.
Let
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}(G)
be the set of cyclic subgroups of
G
.
The map

\pi_{0}R(G)\to\bigoplus_{C\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}(G)}\pi_{0}R(C)\ ,\quad\rho\mapsto\oplus_{C\in\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}(G)}\rho_{|C}

is injective. Indeed, if
\rho
is in the kernel of this map, then
\rho_{|C}=0
for all cyclic subgroups.
Since every
g
in
G
generates a cyclic subgroup we have

{\mathtt{tr}}\rho(g)=0
for all
g
in
G
. This implies
\rho=0
.

We have
\Lambda_{-1}({\mathbb{C}}^{n})=0
for
n\geq 1
.
If
W
is a complex representation of
G
with
W^{G}\not=0
, then

\Lambda_{-1}(W)=\Lambda_{-1}(W^{G}\oplus(W^{G})^{\perp})=\Lambda_{-1}(W^{G})\Lambda_{-1}((W^{G})^{\perp})=0\ .

If
G
is not cyclic, then any
C
in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}(G)
is proper and
V^{C}\not=0
.
This implies
\Lambda_{-1}(V)_{|C}=\Lambda_{-1}(V_{|C})=0
for every
C
in
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}(G)
.
Hence
\Lambda_{-1}(V)=0
.
∎

Proposition 4.13

(
1.14
)
.

If
G
is finite, then
we have an equivalence

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}}\mathbf{Orb}}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}G)

for every
A
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

Proof.

For a family
{\mathcal{F}}
of subgroups of
G
we let
p^{{\mathcal{F}}}:G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}\to*
denote the canonical functor. Then
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}}
is the left Kan extension functor
p^{{\mathcal{F}}}_{!}

along
p^{{\mathcal{F}}}
.

If
{\mathcal{F}}
,
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
are two families of subgroups such that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
, then we let
p_{{\mathcal{F}}}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}:G_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Orb}\to G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}
denote the inclusion.
For the family
\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}
of all subgroups of
G
we get

p^{\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\simeq\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}(*)\simeq\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}G)\ .

For any family
{\mathcal{F}}
of subgroups of
G
the map

c_{G}^{{\mathcal{F}}}:p^{{\mathcal{F}}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\to p^{\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}

(in the domain we omitted restriction of the functor to
G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}
) is induced by the inclusion

G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}\to G_{\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}}\mathbf{Orb}
. If
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}
is a second family such that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\mathcal{F}}
, then
we have a factorization of
c_{G}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}
as

c^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}_{G}:p^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\xrightarrow{c^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}}p^{{\mathcal{F}}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\xrightarrow{c_{G}^{{\mathcal{F}}}}p^{\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\ .

We consider a family
{\mathcal{F}}
of subgroups of
G
containing
\mathcal{C}\mathrm{yc}
. The set of conjugacy classes of subgroups in
{\mathcal{F}}
is a poset. If
[H]
is a maximal element in this poset which is not cyclic, then we can define a new such family
{\mathcal{F}}\setminus{[H]}
by removing
[H]
.

We now assume by induction that

c_{G}^{{\mathcal{F}}}:p^{{\mathcal{F}}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\to p^{\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{G}}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}

is an equivalence and let
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{F}}\setminus[H]
for some maximal conjugacy class
[H]
in
{\mathcal{F}}
.
In order to show that
c_{G}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}
is an equivalence it suffices to show that
c^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}_{{\mathcal{F}}}
is an equivalence.
By the pointwise formula for the left Kan extension we must show for all
G/L
in
G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}
that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Orb}_{/(G/L)}}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\to\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}(G/L)
is an equivalence. The functor
i:L\mathbf{Orb}\to G\mathbf{Orb}
,
S\mapsto G\times_{L}S
induces
an equivalence
L_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L}\mathbf{Orb}\xrightarrow{\simeq}G_{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}}\mathbf{Orb}_{/(G/L)}
, and using

3.14
we can identify
the map in question with
c_{L}^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L}:p^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L}_{!}\mathrm{K}^{L}_{A^{\prime}}\to\mathrm{K}^{L}_{A^{\prime}}(*)
, where
A^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{L}(A)
. If
L\not\in[H]
, then
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L=\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}_{L}
and
c^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L}
is clearly an equivalence.
If
L\in[H]
, then
{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L
is the family of proper subgroups of
L
and
c^{{\mathcal{F}}^{\prime}\cap L}
is an equivalence by

1.13
.
∎

5
The case of cyclic p-groups

5.1
p-completion

Calculating up to
\xi
-completion for
\xi
in
\pi_{0}R(G)
defined by (
4.5
)
based on
1.22
might still be a non-trivial task. If
p
is a prime and one is interested in
p
-torsion phenomena, then it
is more natural to calculate up to
p
-completion. Here we view
p
as a class in

\pi_{0}R(G)
represented by the trivial
p
-dimensional representation of
G
. This allows to apply the language developed in
Section

4.1
.

Recall that the
p
-completion of an abelian group
A
is defined by

\widehat{A}_{p}:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}(\dots\to A/p^{2}A\to A/pA\to A)\ .

Let
E
be in
{\mathbf{Mod}}(R(G))
. The following is well-known.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
\pi_{*}(E)
is degree-wise a finitely generated abelian group, then we
have an isomorphism

\pi_{*}(L_{p}(E))\cong\widehat{\pi_{*}E}_{p}\ .

Proof.

By
4.3
.
3

one knows that the
p
-completion functor is given by the formula

L_{p}(E)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}(\dots\to E/p^{2}\to E/p\to E)\ .

Applying
\pi_{*}
we get the Milnor exact sequence

0\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}{}^{1}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\pi_{*+1}(E/p^{n})\to\pi_{*}(L_{p}(E))\to\widehat{\pi_{*}E}_{p}\to 0\ .

(5.1)

If
\pi_{*}E
is finitely generated, then
\pi_{*}E/p^{n}
is finite for every
n
and the
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}^{1}
-term vanishes.
∎

Let
\xi
be any class in
\pi_{0}R(G)
and consider the endofunctor
(-)[\xi^{-1}]
of a
{\mathbf{Mod}}(R(G))
-module
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
\xi^{n}
is divisible by
p
, then
L_{p}((-)[\xi^{-1}])\simeq 0
.

Proof.

By assumption there exists
\eta
in
\pi_{0}R(G)
such that

\xi^{n}=p\eta
. Let
E
be an object of
{\mathbf{C}}
. Then

p
acts as an equivalence on
E[\xi^{-1}]
. But then

E[\xi^{-1}]
is
p
-acyclic by
4.3
.
2
. ∎

Let now
\xi
be defined by (
4.5
).

Corollary 5.3
.

If there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that
\xi^{n}
is divisible by
p
,
then the assembly map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}\mathbf{Orb}}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}\to\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}G)

becomes an equivalence after
p
-completion.

Proof.

We apply
L_{p}
to the cofibre sequence (
1.9
). By
5.2
and
Theorem

1.12

we get an equivalence

L_{p}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{G_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}\mathbf{Orb}}\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A})\simeq L_{p}(S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}(*)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}L_{p}(\mathrm{K}^{G}_{A}(*))\simeq L_{p}(\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}G))\ .

∎

5.2
Cyclic groups

In this subsection we study the divisibity properties of
\xi
defined in (
4.5
)
for the cyclic group
C_{n}:={\mathbb{Z}}/n{\mathbb{Z}}
for an integer
n
.
The result will be used to show
Theorem

1.23
.

The (algebraic) representation ring of
C_{n}
is given by

\pi_{0}R(C_{n})\cong Z[x]/(x^{n}-1)\ ,

where
x
corresponds to the representation
G\to{\mathtt{Aut}}({\mathbb{C}})
which sends the generator

[1]
to the choice of a primitive
n
’th root of unity
\exp(\frac{2\pi i}{n})
.

Proposition 5.4
.

If
n=p^{k}
for some prime
p
and
k\in{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}
, then
\xi^{2}=p\xi
. Otherwise

\xi^{2}=\xi
and
\xi
is not divisible by any prime.

Proof.

Recall from
Section

4.3
that
V
denotes the complement of the trivial representation in
L^{2}(G)
. Under the identification of the representation ring above
we have
[V]=\sum_{\ell=1}^{n-1}x^{\ell}
and hence
\xi=\Lambda_{-1}(V)=\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(1-x^{\ell})
, see (
4.5
).
We have an injective map of algebras

j:Z[x]/(x^{n}-1)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{C_{n}}

which sends the class of
f
in
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
to the function

j(f):[k]\mapsto f(\exp(\frac{2\pi ik}{n}))
.
Then
\xi
goes to the function

j(\xi):([q]\mapsto\prod_{\ell=1}^{n-1}(1-\exp(\frac{2\pi iq\ell}{n}))\ .

We have

j(\xi)(c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\mbox{$c$ does not generate $C_{n}$}\\
\Phi_{n}(1)&\mbox{$c$ generates $C_{n}$}\end{array}\right.\ ,

where

\Phi_{n}\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]
is the
n
th cyclotomic polynomial.

Lemma 5.5
.

We have

\Phi_{n}(1)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p&\mbox{$n=p^{k}$ for some prime $p$}\\
1&\mbox{$n$ is not a prime power}\end{array}\right.\ .

Proof.

The proof uses some well-known identities for cyclotomic polynomials.
If
n=p^{k}r
such that
p
does not divide
r
, then

\Phi_{n}(x)=\Phi_{pr}(x^{p^{k-1}})
.
In particular we get

\Phi_{n}(1)=\Phi_{pr}(1)
.
We conclude that

\Phi_{n}(1)=\Phi_{q}(1)
, where
q
is the product of the prime divisors of
n
.

If
q
is odd and of the form
pr
with
r\not=1
, then we have

\Phi_{q}(x)=\frac{\Phi_{r}(x^{p})}{\Phi_{r}(x)}
which implies that

\Phi_{q}(1)=1
. We now consider the case

q=2r
for
r\not=1
in which
\Phi_{q}(x)=\Phi_{r}(-x)
. If
r
is an odd prime, then

\Phi_{r}(-1)=1
. Otherwise

r=pr^{\prime}
and
\Phi_{r}(-1)=\frac{\Phi_{r^{\prime}}((-1)^{p})}{\Phi_{r^{\prime}}(-1)}=1
.

If
n=p^{k}
for some
k
and prime
p
, then
q=p
and

\Phi_{n}(1)=p
.
∎

The
5.5
implies that

j(\xi^{2})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}pj(\xi)&\mbox{$n=p^{k}$ for some prime $p$ and $k$ in ${\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}$}\\
j(\xi)&\mbox{else}\end{array}\right.\ .

Since
j
is injective we can conclude that

\xi^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}p\xi&\mbox{$n=p^{k}$ for some prime $p$ and $k$ in ${\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}$}\\
\xi&\mbox{else}\end{array}\right.\ .

In the second case, if
p
would divide
\xi
, then
\xi=p\eta
for some
\eta\in{\mathbb{Z}}[x]/(x^{n}-1)
.
Then we can conclude that
p^{2^{\ell}-1}\eta^{2^{\ell}}=\eta
for any
\ell
in
{\rm Nature}
and therefore
\eta
would be
p
-divisible. But
{\mathbb{Z}}[x]/(x^{n}-1)
does not contain non-trivial
p
-divisible elements.
∎

For a prime
p
and positive integer
k
in
{\rm Nature}
we consider the group
G:=C_{p^{k}}
. We furthermore consider
A
in
\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}
.

Proposition 5.6

(
1.16
)
.

If
p
acts as an equivalence in
A
, then the evaluation

S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*))\to\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)\to\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)[\xi^{-1}]

of the sequence (
1.9
) at
A
splits naturally.

Proof.

By
5.4
we have
\xi^{2}=p\xi
.
We let
\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}[p^{-1}]
be the localizing subcategory of
\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}
on objects on which
p
acts as an equivalence.
On this category we can define the idempotent
\pi:=\frac{\xi}{p}
. Since
A\in\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}[p^{-1}]
we get a decomposition

A\simeq\pi A\oplus(1-\pi)A
.

Now
\xi
acts as an equivalence on
\pi A
(with inverse
\frac{1}{p}
) and annihilates
(1-\pi)A
.
The cofibre sequence

S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*))\to\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)\to\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)[\xi^{-1}]

splits as a sum of the two copies of the cofibre sequences from (
1.9
) applied to
\pi A
and
(1-\pi)A
.
We now have
S_{\xi}(\pi A)\simeq 0
and
(1-\pi)A[\xi^{-1}]\simeq 0
. Adding up what remains we get the desired decomposition

\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)\simeq S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{G}_{(1-\pi)A}(*))\oplus\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{\pi A}(*)[\xi^{-1}]\simeq S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*))\oplus\mathrm{K}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{A}(*)[\xi^{-1}]\ .

∎

Theorem 5.7

(
Theorem

1.23
)
.

For any
A
in
\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}

the assembly map

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{p^{k}}}\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{G}(A))\to\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}C_{p^{k}})

(5.2)

becomes an equivalence after
p
-completion.

Proof.

We perform a similar induction as in the proof of
1.14
over the family of all subgroups of
C_{p^{k}}
. All non-trivial subgroups of
C_{p^{k}}
are groups of the form
C_{p^{k^{\prime}}}
for some
k^{\prime}
between
1
and
k
. For every group of this form we know that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{{{C_{p^{k^{\prime}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\mathrm{rp}}\mathbf{Orb}}}\mathrm{K}_{A^{{}^{\prime}}}^{C_{p^{k^{\prime}}}}\to\mathrm{K}_{A^{{}^{\prime}}}^{C_{p^{k^{\prime}}}}(*)

(with
A^{\prime}:=\mathrm{Res}^{C_{p^{k}}}_{C_{p^{k^{\prime}}}}(A)
)
becomes an equivalence after
p
-completion by
5.3
and
5.4
.
If we repeat the argument from the proof of
1.14
this observation is used in the last line of the argument instead of
1.13
.
The induction ends at the family of the trivial group where we get the statement that the map (
5.2
) becomes an equivalence after
p
-completion.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

If
A\simeq\mathrm{Res}_{C_{p}}(A^{\prime})
for some
A^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{KK}
such that
K_{*}(A^{\prime})
is annihilated by
p^{n}
for some
n
in
{\rm Nature}
, then

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{p}}\mathrm{K}(A)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}C_{p})\ .

(5.3)

Proof.

The values
V^{C_{p}}(S)\rtimes C_{p}
are equivalent to finite sums of copies of
{\mathbf{1}}
and therefore belong to
\mathrm{UCT}
. We can thus apply
3.11
and get an equivalence

\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{A}\simeq\mathrm{K}(A^{\prime})\otimes_{KU}\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}}}\ .

By
Theorem

1.12
the map (
5.3
) is then equivalent to the first map in the fibre sequence

\mathrm{K}(A^{\prime})\otimes_{KU}S_{\xi}(\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}}}(*))\to\mathrm{K}(A^{\prime})\otimes_{KU}\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}}}(*)\to\mathrm{K}(A^{\prime})\otimes_{KU}\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}}}(*)[\xi^{-1}]\ .

(5.4)

Now on the one hand,
p^{n}
annihilates
K_{*}(A^{\prime})
. On the other hand, it acts as an equivalence on
\mathrm{K}^{C_{p}}_{{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}}}(*)[\xi^{-1}]
since
\xi^{2}
is divisible by
p
by
5.4
. It follows that the right-most term in the sequence (
5.4
) is trivial. Consequently its first map is an equivalence.
∎

Recall the definition of the localizing subcategory
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
of
\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p}}
given before the statement of
1.26
.

Proposition 5.9

(
1.26
)
.

If
A
is in
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
, then
the assembly map is an equivalence

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{p}}\mathrm{K}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}C_{p})

(5.5)

Proof.

Since both sides of the assembly map (
) preserve colimits it suffices to show that it is an equivalence for the generators of
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
.

If
A={\mathtt{Ind}}^{C_{p}}(A^{\prime\prime})
for some
A^{\prime\prime}
in
\mathrm{KK}
, then the assertion is an instance of
1.10
.
In the second case where
A=\mathrm{Res}_{C_{p}}(A^{\prime})
for some
A^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{KK}
such that
\mathrm{K}_{*}(A^{\prime})
is annihilated by
p^{n}
for some
n
in
{\rm Nature}
the assertion is given by
5.8
.
∎

5.3
Some calculations

In the following
p
is a prime,
k
is in
{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}
,
H
is a subgroup of
C_{p^{k}}
,
B:={\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}/\ell
in
\mathrm{KK}^{H}
for an integer
\ell
with
p\not|\ell
, and
A:=B^{\otimes C_{p^{k}}/H}
.

Lemma 5.10

(Lemma
1.19
)
.

We have

\mathrm{K}(A\rtimes_{r}C_{p^{k}})[\xi^{-1}]\simeq R(C_{p^{k}})[\xi^{-1}]/\ell\simeq(KU/\ell)^{\oplus(p-1)p^{k}}\ .

Proof.

In view of
Theorem

1.12
we have an equivalence

K(A\rtimes_{r}C_{p^{k}})[\xi^{-1}]\simeq\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}(A)\ .

We now employ
3.18
stating that the composition
\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}\circ(-)^{\otimes C_{p^{k}}/H}:\mathrm{KK}^{H}\to{\mathbf{Mod}}(R(C_{p^{k}}))
preserves all colimits for the marked equivalence in

\hskip-14.22636pt\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}(A)\simeq\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}(({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}/\ell)^{\otimes C_{p^{k}}/H})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{H}}^{\otimes C_{p^{k}}/H})/\ell\simeq\mathrm{Cof}^{C_{p^{k}}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}^{C_{p^{k}}}})/\ell\simeq R(C_{p^{k}})[\xi^{-1}]/\ell\ .

As a
KU
-module the second summand in (
1.10
)
is the given by
(KU/\ell)^{\oplus(p-1)p^{k}}
, where
(p-1)p^{k}
is the number of generators of
C_{p^{k}}
.
∎

We now restrict to the case of
k=1
and the trivial subgroup
H
.

Lemma 5.11

(
1.20
)
.

We have

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{p}}\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{C_{p}}(A))\simeq(KU/\ell)^{n_{0}(p)}\oplus(\Sigma KU/\ell)^{n_{1}(p)}

with

n_{i}(p):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{2^{p-2}+\frac{(p+1)(p-1)}{2}}{p}&i=0\\
\frac{2^{p-2}-\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{2}}{p}&i=1\end{array}\right.

for
p>2
and

n_{i}(2):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&i=0\\
0&i=1\end{array}\right.

Proof.

We have a cofibre sequence

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}\to{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/\ell

which shows that

{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/\ell
belongs to the
\mathrm{UCT}
class.
Since
\mathrm{K}
is symmetric monoidal on the
\mathrm{UCT}
-class we have an equivalence

\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{C_{p}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/\ell)^{\otimes C_{p}})\simeq\mathrm{K}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/\ell)^{\otimes C_{p}}\simeq(KU/\ell)^{\otimes C_{p}}\ ,

where the second and third term have the
C_{p}
-action by permutation of tensor factors, and the tensor product is interpreted in
{\mathbf{Mod}}(KU)
. We use the equivalence of
\mathrm{ho}({\mathbf{Mod}}(KU))
and the homotopy category
\mathrm{ho}(D^{2\mathrm{per}}({\mathbb{Z}}))
of the
2
-periodic derived category of
{\mathbb{Z}}
. Under this equivalence the cofibre sequence
KU\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}KU\to KU/\ell
goes to the cofibre sequence
{\mathbb{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{Z}}\to{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
. So
KU/\ell
is realized by the chain complex
{\mathbb{Z}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{Z}}
where the first copy of
{\mathbb{Z}}
is in degree
-1
.

We can therefore calculate
\pi_{*}((KU/\ell)^{\otimes C_{p}})
with the induced
C_{p}
-action as the
C_{2}
-graded homology of the tensor product of complexes

({\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\ell}{\mathbb{Z}})^{\otimes C_{p}}
. As a first simplification we note that

{\mathbb{Z}}\xrightarrow{\ell}{\mathbb{Z}}
is equivalent to
{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]
. Since now
p
is invertible on the chain groups we can interchange the order of taking homology and
C_{p}
-coinvariants on
({\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ell}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}])^{\otimes C_{p}}
.

Let us start with then case
p=2
. Then the resulting chain complex is (starting on degree
-2
)

{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]\to{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]\oplus{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]\to{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]\ ,

where

C_{2}
acts trivially on chain group in degree
-1
, by the flip of summands on the group in degree
-1
, and by the sign representation on the summand in degree
-2
. After taking coinvariants we get a chain complex

{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{-1}}}{{\to}}{\mathbb{Z}}[2^{-1}]
whose differential is multiplication by
\ell
.
We conclude that the odd homology vanishes and the even homology is given by
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell
.
This shows that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{2}}\mathrm{K}(\widehat{\mathrm{Res}}^{C_{2}}(A))\simeq KU/\ell

as asserted.

We now consider the case
p>2
.
We write

({\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]\to{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}])^{\otimes C_{p}}\cong(Z_{-p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{-p}}}{{\to}}\dots Z_{1-p}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1-p}}}{{\to}}\dots\to Z_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{-1}}}{{\to}}Z_{0})\ ,

where
Z_{-k}
are free
{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]
-modules with a
C_{p}
-action. In detail

Z_{-k}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{triv}^{C_{p}}({\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}])&k=0,p\\
{\mathtt{Ind}}^{C_{p}}({\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]^{\oplus\frac{1}{p}\binom{p}{k}})&k\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}\end{array}\right.\ .

The
C_{p}
-coinvariants
Z_{-k,C_{p}}:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{BC_{p}}Z_{-k}
of the chain groups are therefore given by

Z_{-k,C_{p}}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]&k=0,p\\
{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]^{\oplus\frac{1}{p}\binom{p}{k}}&k\in\{1,\dots,p-1\}\end{array}\right.\ .

(5.6)

If we divide the differentials of the resulting chain complex

0\to Z_{-p,C_{p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{-p}}}{{\to}}Z_{1-p,C_{p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{1-p}}}{{\to}}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d_{-1}}}{{\to}}Z_{0,C_{p}}\to 0

(5.7)

of coinvariants by
\ell
,
then we get the analogous complex of coinvariants for
({\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}]\xrightarrow{1}{\mathbb{Z}}[p^{-1}])^{\otimes C_{p}}
which is clearly exact. Since the chain groups are
\ell
-torsion free we can conclude that the homology of
the complex (
5.7
) are sums of copies of
{\mathbb{Z}}/\ell{\mathbb{Z}}
. It remains to determine the ranks.
To this end we calculate the ranks
r_{i}
of the differentials
d_{-i}
. Since the complex (
5.7
) is exact after inverting
\ell
we see that

r_{j}=\sum_{i=0}^{j-1}(-1)^{i-j+1}{\mathtt{rk}}Z_{-i,C_{p}}\ .

Inserting (
5.6
) we get for
j<p

r_{j}=\frac{(-1)^{j+1}}{p}\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}(-1)^{i}\binom{p}{i}+(-1)^{j+1}=\frac{\binom{p-1}{j-1}+(-1)^{j}(1-p)}{p}\ .

We conclude that

{\mathtt{rk}}H_{-j}(\eqref{ewqdqwedewdqewd})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{\binom{p-1}{j}+(-1)^{j-1}(1-p)}{p}&j\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}\\
0&j=p\end{array}\right.

We now must add up the even and odd parts.
We use

\sum_{j=0}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\binom{p-1}{2j}=\sum_{j=0}^{\frac{p-3}{2}}\binom{p-1}{2j+1}=2^{p-2}

and get the asserted numbers

\displaystyle n_{0}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{p-2}+\frac{(p+1)(p-1)}{2}}{p}

\displaystyle n_{1}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{p-2}-\frac{(p-1)^{2}}{2}}{p}\ .

∎

Lemma 5.12

(
1.28
)
.

We have
({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/p)^{\otimes C_{p}}\in{\mathbf{G}}(p)
.

Proof.

We realize the cofibre sequence
{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}\to{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/p
as the image under
\mathrm{kk}
of
some exact sequence of separable
C^{*}
-algebras

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
. So
\mathrm{kk}(A)\simeq\mathrm{kk}(B)\simeq{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}
and
\mathrm{kk}(C)\simeq{\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/p
. We can then
employ the decreasing filtration
(I_{m})_{m=0,\dots,p}
of
B^{\otimes C_{p}}

from step
3
of the proof of
2.19
.
We have
\mathrm{kk}^{C_{p}}(I_{p})\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{C_{p}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}^{\otimes C_{p}})\simeq\mathrm{Res}_{C_{p}}({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}})
which shows that

I_{p}
belongs to
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
. By the same argument
\mathrm{kk}^{C_{p}}(I_{0})\in{\mathbf{G}}(p)
.
By (
3.21
) all quotients
I_{m}/I_{m+1}
for
1=0,\dots,m-1
are induced
from the trivial subgroup and therefore also belong to
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
.
We can therefore argue by
induction that
\mathrm{kk}^{C_{p}}(I_{k})
belongs to
{\mathbf{G}}(p)
for every
k
in
\{1,\dots p\}
.
This implies that

({\mathbf{1}}_{\mathrm{KK}}/p)^{\otimes C_{p}}\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{C_{p}}(I_{0}/I_{1})\in{\mathbf{G}}(p)
.
∎
